# Alban Thomas Atwood.

---

<meta>Faint pencil numeral '6' written above the crest.</meta>

->This is the labor, this is the work<-
<note>original Latin: "Hic labor hoc opus est." This is a famous quotation from Virgil’s *Aeneid* (Book VI), referring to the immense difficulty of returning from the underworld. In an alchemical context, it signifies the arduous nature of the "Great Work" of spiritual transformation.</note>

<image-desc>A printed bookplate featuring heraldic and symbolic imagery. At the top, a flowing banner contains the Latin motto translated above. Below the banner is a crest depicting a dragon or wyvern’s head with a protruding tongue, emerging from a ducal coronet. Centered below the crest is a large <term>Ouroboros—a serpent forming a circle by consuming its own tail—</term>which symbolizes eternal return and the unity of all things. It encloses a radiant sixteen-pointed star. The entire arrangement is framed within a rectangular plate impression.</image-desc>

->### Thomas South.<-
<note>Thomas South (1785–1855) was a prominent researcher of Hermetic philosophy. He and his daughter, Mary Anne Atwood (née South), are best known for their collaborative work on *A Suggestive Inquiry into the Hermetic Mystery*, a foundational text of the alchemical revival.</note>

<summary>This page displays the symbolic bookplate of Thomas South, featuring the Ouroboros and a motto from Virgil, signaling the alchemical and scholarly nature of the manuscript's provenance.</summary>
<keywords>Alban Thomas Atwood, Thomas South, bookplate, heraldry, Ouroboros, Hermeticism, Virgil, alchemy, ex-libris</keywords>

It advertises a collection that covers the transformation of metals, the "three kingdoms of nature," and biographies of famous alchemical philosophers.</summary>

# To the Reader.

## Highly Favored Reader!

Because I have deemed it necessary to have a new title printed for the First Part of my <term>Bibliotheca Chymica</term> <note>original: "Bibliotheca Chymica"; a Latin title meaning "Chemical Library," referring to a comprehensive bibliography of books on alchemy and chemistry</note>, and since the empty space here allows for it regardless, I intended to give the Highly Favored Reader news of a curious manuscript; which lies ready for printing, and should enthusiasts be found for it, shall appear as soon as possible, the title and contents of which entire work are as follows:

in the Roth-Scholtz Library. <note>Friedrich Roth-Scholtz (1687–1736) was a prominent Nuremberg bookseller and publisher who compiled extensive bibliographies of alchemical and hermetic literature.</note>

# Contents of the Entire Work.

The same treats the following five questions in as many chapters.

Chapter I. Whether the **transmutation of metals is possible?** <note>original: "Verwandlung der Metallen." While "Verwandlung" means "transformation" or "change," "transmutation" is the specific technical term used in alchemy for changing base metals into gold or silver.</note>
Chap. II. Whether **transmutation has actually occurred anywhere?**
Chap. III. Whether one has any **experiments** <note>original Latin: "Experimenta"</note> from which the **possibility of the transmutation of metals** can be inferred?
Chap. IV. **What is to be thought of the Universal Medicine, Potable Gold, and similar things?** <note>original: "Medicina Universali" and "Auro potabili." The Universal Medicine (Panacea) and Drinkable Gold were legendary alchemical preparations believed to cure all ailments and grant longevity.</note>
Chap. V. **Whether Alchemy is to be advised to anyone, and especially to great lords?** <note>original: "grossen Herren." This refers to the historical reality where alchemists sought the financial patronage of princes and kings, often with risky results.</note>

Every chapter is divided into its sections; from these are the following summaries, and thus a

## Brief Presentation

of the entire work.

### Chapter I. Whether the transmutation of metals is possible?

§. 1. Why this question is being treated? §. 2. The first objection against the transmutation of metals: *The species of things are not changed among themselves.* <note>original Latin: "Species rerum inter se non mutantur." This was a standard Aristotelian philosophical argument used by critics to claim that one substance (like lead) could never become another (like gold).</note> §. 3. The response to the first objection. §. 4. The other objection concerning the disagreement of the philosophers? §. 5. The response to this objection. §. 6. The 3rd objection derived from the vain labors and deceptions of the alchemists. <note>The author refers to "laboribus" and "betrügereyen"—acknowledging the many "puffers" or fraudulent alchemists who gave the field a bad name.</note> §. 7. The response to the objections contained in the 6th section. §. 8. The 4th objection: God

## Chapter II. Whether the transformation of metals has occurred anywhere?

§. 1. Regarding the difficulty of answering this question. §. 2. Regarding the Elector **Augustus** of Saxony, his wife **Anna**, and Prince **Christian**, and how all three of them, along with Sebald Schwärzer, etc., understood the art. <note>Augustus I (1526–1586) and Anna of Denmark were famous for their "Gold House" in Dresden where they practiced alchemy.</note> §. 3. Why one generally achieves nothing in imitating the Electoral Saxon processes? §. 4. Whether S. Schwärzer was an *adept* <note>original Latin: "Adeptus" — a master who has successfully created the Philosopher's Stone.</note>, or a fraud and a murderer? §. 5. How this art departed from the Electoral House of Saxony? §. 6. The story of David Beuter. §. 7. Regarding Emperor **Rudolf II’s** arts, the great treasure he left behind, and his valet, etc. <note>Rudolf II (1552–1612) was the Holy Roman Emperor who famously moved his court to Prague, making it a sanctuary for alchemists like Tycho Brahe and Johannes Kepler.</note> §. 8. Regarding the Englishman **Kelley** at the court of **Rudolf**, his companion **John Dee**, and the discovery of the red and white <term>tincture</term> <note>The "tincture" is the transformative substance; "red" was believed to create gold, and "white" created silver.</note> in an English Bishop's grave, etc. §. 9. Regarding Emperor **Rudolf’s** <term>tincture</term>, and other proofs that he became an *adept*. §. 10. Regarding **Gustenhofer’s** *projection* <note>original: "projection." The act of casting the alchemical powder onto molten base metal to transform it into gold.</note> and his fates. §. 11. Regarding what occurred under and during the reign of Emperor **Ferdinand II** through Sendivogius. <note>Michael Sendivogius (1566–1636) was a famous Polish alchemist and diplomat.</note> §. 12. Regarding what occurred under and during the reign of Emperor **Ferdinand III**, and elsewhere through Richthausen or Baron **Chaos**, and the large golden *coin* <note>original Latin: "Numo." This refers to a specific commemorative medal struck from "alchemical gold" in 1648 to prove the success of the experiment.</note>, which was struck as an eternal memorial and proof of the *transmutation* that took place. §. 13. Regarding what occurred under and during the reign of Emperor **Leopold I** with Father Wenzel or the Baron von

Of **ALEXANDER SETON**, and the many *specimens* <note>original Latin: "Speciminibus" — demonstrations or physical proofs.</note> of the gold-making art shown here and there, and the *fates* <note>original Latin: "fatis" — the life stories or ultimate destinies.</note> of both him and **SENDIVOGIUS**. <note>Alexander Seton, known as "The Cosmopolite," was a 17th-century alchemist who was imprisoned and tortured for his secrets; Michael Sendivogius was his pupil who rescued him.</note> §. 34. Of the *particular* <note>A "particular" is a successful alchemical transformation of a specific base metal into gold or silver, though it lacks the "universal" power of the true Philosopher's Stone.</note> demonstrated in Stockholm by Lieutenant-Colonel **PAYKUL**, as well as the *particular* of **Paul Hübner** in Danzig. §. 35. Likewise, of Mr. **DIPPEL’S** tincture, 50 parts produced according to **FABRI’S** process, and §. 36. of the tincture gifted by **Baron Schmolz**, which acted upon 600 parts. §. 37. **Dr. KUNDMANN’S** testimony regarding the transformation of lead into gold, as well as **Dr. Ettner’s** account of the *Stone* <note>original Latin: "Lapide" — the Philosopher's Stone.</note> itself. §. 38. **Dr. OLAUS BORRICHIUS’S** description of the transmutation that occurred at the house of Mr. **Gersdorff** in Amsterdam. §. 39. Of the curious philosophical *arborification* <note>The "Philosophical Tree," a chemical reaction where metals grow into branch-like structures, often seen as a sign of the metal's "life."</note> performed by a man from Liège at the home of Mr. **SANSIMON** in Brussels. §. 40. Of the bright *philosophical light* ignited by a Burgundian at the house of Mr. von **Nesmir** in Leuven. §. 41. Of **du BOIS** and his transformation of a musket ball into gold in the presence of King **LOUIS XIII** and Cardinal **RICHELIEU**. §. 45. ### Chapter III. Concerning the Experiments from which the possibility of the transformation of metals can be inferred.

§. 1. Why certain experiences are cited.
§. 2. The most certain experiments flow *from the analysis of metals* <note>original: "ex Analysi metallorum"</note>.
§. 3. Concerning different ways to *resolve* <note>To "resolve" a metal meant to break it down into its constituent parts or "first matter."</note> metals.

# In the Roth-Scholtz Library
<note>Friedrich Roth-Scholtz (1687–1736) was a prominent Nuremberg bookseller and bibliographer who compiled vast collections of alchemical literature.</note>

->Page 11<-

§. 4. **BASILIUS VALENTINUS’S** <note>The legendary, possibly pseudonymous, 15th-century Benedictine monk regarded as a master of antimony and the Great Work.</note> *Anatomy of Metals* <note>original: "Anatomia metallorum"</note> according to Mr. **FRIEDRICH CLINGE**.

§. 5. The decomposition of gold, etc., according to **ABRAHAM RIESE’S** process with mercurial solvents <note>original: "☿almenstruis" — "menstrua" were solvents used in alchemy to dissolve solids.</note>.

§. 6. Mechanical experience in wire-drawing and gilding.

§. 7. What can be inferred from the coloring of the water, and how Dr. **MÜLLER** is to be understood in this regard.

§. 8. A small piece of native sulfur <note>original: "selbst-gewachsenen Schwefels" — sulfur found in its pure, natural state rather than extracted from ore.</note> that transmuted silver into gold.

§. 9. A certain *aqua fortis* <term>Nitric acid; literally "strong water," used to part gold from silver.</term> matures much silver into gold.

§. 10. A chemist in Amsterdam transmutes mercury <note>original: "☿um"</note> into silver with sulfur.

§. 11. A certain spirit of salt <term>Hydrochloric acid.</term> matures lead into gold and silver.

§. 12. Someone was able to transmute silver into gold in three hours with common salt.

§. 13. Fixed Silver <note>original: "Luna fixa" — silver that has been chemically treated so that it no longer dissolves in nitric acid, thus "mimicking" gold.</note> can be made through salt cementation <note>A process of heating a metal in contact with a powdered reagent.</note>.

§. 14. Gold is obtained from copper using sulfur.

§. 15. Baron **SCHRÖDER’S** two experiments with mercury sublimate <note>original: "☿us sublimatus" — corrosive sublimate.</note> and cinnabar <note>Mercury sulfide.</note>.

§. 16. Silver becomes gold with the soul <note>original: "anima" — the vital essence or "tincture" extracted from a metal.</note> extracted from gold.

§. 17. The sublimate made from silver with sal ammoniac <note>original: "Salmiac"</note> transmutes silver <note>original: "Lunam"</note> into gold.

§. 18. **CONDRESYANI’S** or **BASILIUS VALENTINUS’S** experiment to make gold from mercury and silver.

§. 19. **LAURENTIUS MEISNER’S** precipitate from silver into gold.

§. 20. Two remarkable experiments with the sublimate of tin <note>original: "♃is"</note> and copper <note>original: "♀is"</note>; likewise with the mercury of tin and gold from *aqua regia* <term>A mixture of nitric and hydrochloric acids capable of dissolving gold.</term> according to **GLAUBER** <note>Johann Rudolph Glauber (1604–1670), a famous German-Dutch alchemist and chemist known for "Glauber's Salt."</note>.

§. 21. Metallic spirits and gilding prove the possibility of the Art <note>The "Art" refers to Alchemy or the transmutation of metals.</note>.

<meta>catchword: Chapter [Caput]</meta>

<summary>This page lists paragraphs 4 through 21 of a bibliography or index, focusing on specific alchemical recipes and experimental "proofs" for transmuting base metals into gold and silver. It cites prominent figures like Basilius Valentinus and Johann Rudolph Glauber, as well as specific chemical agents like aqua regia and spirit of salt.</summary>

<keywords>Basilius Valentinus, Abraham Riese, aqua fortis, spirit of salt, fixed silver, mercury sublimate, cinnabar, Johann Rudolph Glauber, transmutation, metallic spirits, gilding</keywords>

Catalogue of Chemical Books

## *Chapter* IV. Concerning the *Universal Medicine*, *Potable Gold*, etc.

§. 1. The twofold powers of the *Stone* <note>original: "Lapidis" — referring to the Philosophers' Stone, which was believed to have the power to both "heal" base metals into gold and heal the human body of all infirmity.</note> upon *Metals* and humans.

§. 2. Division of this treatise into two questions.

§. 3. Boastful claims <note>original: "Rodomontaden" — derived from the character Rodomonte in Italian epic poetry, referring to the vainglorious or exaggerated promises often made by "puffers" or fraudulent alchemists.</note> concerning the *Universal Medicine*.

§. 4. Sigismund Schwärtzer’s <note>A 16th-century alchemist who served the Elector of Saxony and claimed to have achieved the transmutation of gold.</note> opinion on the powers of the *Philosophers' Stone* <note>original: "Lapidis Philos."</note> in *Medicine*.

§. 5. What is actually to be understood by a *Universal Medicine*?

§. 6. That there is no such *Universal Medicine* for the human *body* <note>original: "Corpora"</note> as there is for metals.

§. 7. Concerning the science upon which human life *depends*, and the art of preserving it.

§. 8. Whether the *matter* <note>original: "Materia"</note> of light is the spirit of life?

§. 9. *Phosphorus* <note>original: "phosphori" — at this time, this referred generally to various luminescent substances, including the newly discovered element, believed by many to contain the "spark" of life.</note> and other similar *substances* do not demonstrate a *vital flame* <note>original: "flammulam vitalem" — the internal fire thought to sustain biological life.</note>.

§. 10. Whether there are intermediary spirits between the soul and the body?

§. 11. The *proponents* <note>original: "Patroni"</note> of these [spirits] may well *admit* the possibility of a *Universal Medicine*.

§. 12. Those who do not *admit* an intermediary essence <note>original: "mitleres Wesen"</note> may well *deny* the *Universal Medicine*, and give a *reason* as to why it cannot *perpetuate* human life.

§. 13. Whether and how one might *argue* for a *Universal Medicine* based on the rapidly penetrating power of poison?

§. 14. What should be the nature of a true *Universal Medicine*?

§. 15. Wherein the *Universal Medicine* is to be sought?

§. 16. Whether it must be *prepared* <note>original: "præpariret"</note> from non-specific things?

<meta>catchword: §. 17. Whether</meta>

<summary>This page outlines the theoretical framework for Chapter IV, debating the existence and nature of a "Universal Medicine" that could heal both metals and the human body. It explores the philosophical intersection of chemistry and biology, specifically questioning whether a "vital flame" or intermediary spirits sustain life.</summary>

<keywords>Universal Medicine, Potable Gold, Philosophers' Stone, Sigismund Schwärtzer, vital flame, phosphorus, medical alchemy, life extension, intermediary spirits</keywords>

§. 17. Whether and how a medicine may be prepared from gold?

§. 18. Whether corrosive substances and the medicines prepared from them can also be applied internally to the human body with benefit? <note>Alchemists often debated whether "corrosives" like strong acids used to dissolve metals remained toxic even after being processed into medicine.</note>

§. 19. Concerning the **potable gold of FRANCIS ANTHONY.** <note>Francis Anthony (1550–1623) was an English physician who famously promoted "Aurum Potabile" as a panacea, leading to many disputes with the College of Physicians.</note>

§. 20. Concerning the **potable gold of THEOPHRASTUS PARACELSUS.**

§. 21. Concerning the **potable gold** of Mr. **NEUDI.**

§. 22. Concerning **potable gold** <note>original: "auro potabili"</note> prepared with sweet salt crystals.

§. 23. Concerning **potable Sun & Moon** <note>original: "Sole & Luna potabili" — "Sun" and "Moon" are the alchemical names for gold and silver.</note> prepared with the **fixed microcosmic salt.** <note>original: "Sale fixo microcosmico" — a salt typically derived from human urine, believed by alchemists to contain the "spirit" of the microcosm/man.</note>

§. 24. Concerning the **Sweet Essence of Halle.** <note>original: "Essentia dulci Halensi" — a famous gold-based proprietary medicine developed in Halle, Germany, in the early 18th century.</note>

§. 25. Concerning **Potable Gold & Silver** according to **KUNCKEL’S** description. <note>Refers to Johann von Kunckel, a celebrated 17th-century chemist and glassmaker.</note>

§. 26. Concerning gold brought to a **neutral salt** <note>original: "Sale enixo" — a term used by chemists like Boyle and Becher for a salt that is neither purely acid nor alkali.</note> and then extracted.

§. 27. Concerning **Dr. BECHER’S solar panacea.** <note>Johann Joachim Becher was a famous 17th-century scholar and alchemist whose "Solar" (gold-based) cure-all was highly regarded.</note>

§. 28. Concerning gold and silver brought to **solubility** <note>original: "Solubilitåt"</note> without corrosives, and the operations serving that purpose.

§. 29. Concerning other **authors** <note>original: "Auctoribus"</note> who have written on these matters. With a reminder on the philosophical preparation of **vegetables** <note>original: "Vegetabilien" — in this context, referring to plants and herbs</note> for medicine.

§. 30. Concerning gold brought into the nature of a vegetable, and the resulting best and most pleasant **potable gold** for human nature.

## Chapter V. Whether **Alchemy** is to be advised to anyone?

§. 1. That from a few examples, one cannot draw a conclusion about others.

Concerning the praises <note>original: "elogiis"</note> of alchemy given by **PENOTUS** and **DU CLOS** at the end of their lives. <note>Bernard Gilles Penot (c. 1519–1617) and Samuel Cottereau du Clos (1598–1685) were both famous alchemists who, after decades of study and significant financial loss, left behind complex reflections on the art—Penot famously warned others away from the pursuit despite his lifelong devotion to it.</note>

Erhard Cellius was a scholar who recorded these courtly activities.</note>

--- Page 22 ---
<meta>This page is the "Vorrede" (Preface) of the work. It follows the title page, which identified the book as the first part of Friedrich Roth-Scholtz’s library catalogue. The preface establishes the author's intent and defines his broad scope for the term "chemical books."</meta>

Preface.

# Preface.

## Highly Esteemed Reader!

Since I find myself still prevented from publishing my **CHEMICAL LIBRARY** <note>original Latin: **BIBLIOTHECAM CHEMICAM**</note>, which I have in hand, according to my wishes; wherein not only the differences between all various editions, but also all chemical books in general are to be evaluated; I have therefore decided, at the request of good friends, to have a brief catalogue thereof printed until that time, of which this first part now appears, and the remaining parts shall soon follow.

By the words "chemical books," I do not mean only those which treat solely of the Philosopher’s Stone <term>Philosopher’s Stone: In alchemy, a legendary substance capable of turning base metals into gold and silver; also used as an elixir of life.</term> or the transmutation of base metals into better ones; but also all other books which have been written about the three kingdoms of nature <note>The "three kingdoms" refers to the traditional classification of natural objects into mineral, vegetable, and animal.</note> in general, such as: mining, herbs, animals, thermal baths, the art of domestic economy <note>German: *Haushaltungs-Kunst*, referring to the management of a household or estate, often including agriculture and practical chemistry.</note>, and so forth.

Most of these books, both old and new, can be found by enthusiasts at the shop of the heirs of the late <note>German: *seel.*, short for *selig*, meaning "blessed" or "deceased."</note> Mr. Johann Daniel Tauber in Nuremberg next to the Schuster-Gasse <note>Shoemaker’s Lane.</note>, or also in Altdorf in the Tauber bookshop at fair prices, alongside many other books.

->Nuremberg,<-
->February 5th, 1725.<-

<meta>catchword: De</meta>

<summary>In this preface, Friedrich Roth-Scholtz explains that while his full chemical bibliography is delayed, he is releasing this shorter catalogue. He defines "chemical books" broadly to include mining, botany, zoology, and domestic science, reflecting the 18th-century view of chemistry as the study of all natural materials.</summary>

<keywords>Friedrich Roth-Scholtz, chemical bibliography, Philosopher's Stone, three kingdoms of nature, 18th-century science, Nuremberg, book trade, Johann Daniel Tauber</keywords>

**Chemical Miscarriage** <note>original Latin: *Abortus Chymicus*</note>, or the **Valleys of the Secrets of Divine Wisdom**: That is, a **Philosophical Discourse** on the **Philosopher’s Stone** and its wonderful birth, directed toward the **Basilian practice** <note>Referring to the methods of the legendary alchemist Basil Valentine.</note>. 4to. Halle, 1659.

**Acts of the Berlin Physicians**, collected and arranged for the advancement of art and science. <note>original Latin: *Acta Medicorum Berolinensium, in incrementum artis & Scientiarum collecta & digesta*</note> 8vo.

**The Real Adept** <term>Adept: A person who has successfully achieved the "Great Work" of alchemy, specifically the creation of the Philosopher’s Stone.</term>, that is, the brief dedication of a true **Adept** to all lovers of **Alchemy**, consisting firstly of a heartfelt warning against all sorts of deceptive metal and mineral processes, as well as "special" and "particular" processes <term>Particular processes: Alchemical experiments aimed at transforming specific amounts of metal or achieving limited results, rather than the universal transformation offered by the Philosopher's Stone.</term>. 2. A thorough proof that not only did a gold-producing wonder-matter or **Philosopher’s Stone** exist in the past; but it can still be found and prepared by various people today. 3. A sincere confession by the author that through Divine Grace he not only possesses such a secret; but is also resolved to ocularly <note>Through visual demonstration.</note> demonstrate and reveal it to several honest patriots. 8vo. Leipzig, 1715. See **Kastell**.

**Georg Agricola’s** **Mining Book**, etc. with clean wood-cut figures. folio. Basel, 1621.

**Georg Andreas Agricola’s** well-founded experiment on the **Universal Propagation** of all trees, shrubs, and flowering plants. Adorned with clean copperplates. folio. Leipzig, 1716.

# Catalogue of Chemical Books

...observations and remarks <note>original Latin: *...vationum & Animadversionum*</note> on Johann Popp’s **Chemical Medicine**, in which all processes are diligently examined and corrected, and also expanded with several new processes. Parts 1 and 2. 4to. Leipzig, 1639.
**The same work by the same author.** With Johann Helfrich Juncken’s notes. 3 parts. 4to. Nuremberg, 1686.
**Agricola, Johann Georg.** Detailed description of the entire living stag. 4to. Amberg, 1617. <note>This work by a namesake of the famous mineralogist focuses on the medicinal uses of deer parts.</note>
**Agricola, Georg.** Book on subterranean creatures <note>original Latin: *de Animantibus subterraneis*</note>. 8vo. Basel, from the Froben workshop, 1549.
**By the same author.** A treatise on metallurgy <note>original Latin: *de Re metallica*</note>, now first published after the author’s careful revision and correction. With a nomenclature of metallurgical terms. 8vo. Leipzig, 1546. <note>Agricola is often called the "Father of Mineralogy"; this is one of the most important early texts on mining and smelting.</note>
**By the same author.** Five books on the **Origin and Causes of Subterranean Things**; four books on the **Nature of those things which flow out of the earth**; ten books on the **Nature of Fossils** <term>Fossils: In the 16th century, this term referred to anything "dug up" from the earth, including minerals and gemstones, rather than just prehistoric remains.</term>; two books on **Ancient and New Metals**. *Bermannus*, or a dialogue on metallurgy. A German interpretation of metallurgical terms. Added is a most fruitful index, illustrated with marginal notes by Johann Siegfried. Also included are various and learned observations on metallurgical things and names from the papers of Georg Fabricius, in which those matters are primarily explained. 8vo. Wittenberg, 1612.
**Agrippa, Heinrich Cornelius.** An invective declamation on the **Uncertainty and Vanity of the Sciences**, in which all that "war of the giants" <note>original Latin: *Giganthomachia*</note> of the Sophists is attacked with more than Herculean boldness; it is taught that nothing certain, eternal, or divine lies hidden anywhere except in the solid words of God and the preeminence of God's Word. 8vo. Published by Eucharius of Cologne <note>original Latin: *apud Eucharium Agrippinatem*</note>, 1531. <note>Agrippa was a famous polymath and occultist; this work is a skeptical critique of human knowledge.</note>
**By the same author.** Commentaries on the **Short Art of Ramon Lull**. With various figures. 8vo. Cologne, 1533. <term>Short Art: A system of logic and mnemonics developed by the medieval mystic Ramon Lull, using rotating circles to combine concepts.</term>
**By the same author.** Ten Orations, namely: I. On the reading of Plato’s *Banquet*. II. On the reading of Hermes Trismegistus, on the power and wisdom of God. III. On behalf of a certain doctoral candidate. IV. To the Lords of Metz, when he was accepted as their Advocate, Syndic, and Orator. V. To the Senate of Luxembourg, on behalf of the Lords...

### in the Roth-Scholtz Library.

...delivered to his people of Metz <note>original Latin: *suis Metensibus habita*</note>. VI. Written as a greeting for a certain prince and bishop on behalf of the Lords of Metz. VII. Written as a greeting for a certain magnificent man on behalf of the Lords of Metz. VIII. Delivered by a certain relative of his, a Carmelite and "formed" Bachelor of Sacred Theology <note>A "formed bachelor" was a candidate who had completed all required coursework and was entering the final stage of their degree.</note>, upon accepting a Regency in Paris. IX. Delivered for the son of Christian <note>referring to King Christian II</note>, the Most Serene King of Denmark, Norway, and Sweden, etc., upon the arrival of the Emperor. X. Delivered at the funeral of Lady Margaret, Princess of the Austrians and Burgundians <note>Margaret of Austria (1480–1530), who served as the Governor of the Habsburg Netherlands.</note>. A brief history by the same author regarding the double coronation of Emperor Charles V at Bologna. Epigrams by the same author and other learned men. 8vo. Cologne, 1535.

**The same author's Fourth Book on Occult Philosophy, or on Magical Ceremonies.** To which are added the *Magical Elements* of Pietro d'Abano. 8vo. Cologne, 1565. <note>Though attributed to Agrippa, this "Fourth Book" is considered a spurious work by modern scholars, likely published after his death to capitalize on the success of his trilogy on occult philosophy.</note>

**Agricola, Georg.** Five books on the **Measures and Weights of the Romans and Greeks**; two books on foreign measures and weights. To which Andreas Alciatus <note>A famous Italian jurist and pioneer of the emblem book.</note> has again disputed concerning measures and weights; a brief defense in one book. One book on the restoration of weights and measures; three books on the price of metals and coins. Folio. Basel, 1550. From the Froben workshop.

**Ten books by the same author on the Nature of Fossils.** <term>Fossils: In the 16th century, this term was used for any mineral, metal, or stone "dug up" from the earth, not exclusively the remains of prehistoric life.</term> With the treatise *Bermannus*, or *On Metallurgy*. Folio. [Basel], 1550. See *Agricola on Measures*.

**Agrippa, Heinrich Cornelius.** A book on the **Uncertainty and Vanity of All Sciences and Arts**, a truly pleasant and elegant read. Two small books by the same author are added, one of which is on the nobility and excellence of the female sex, and its superiority over the male; the other on marriage or wedlock, also most pleasant to read. 12mo. Cologne, 1584, 1598, and 1622.

**The same work by the same author.** 12mo. Frankfurt and Leipzig, published by the Plener heirs, 1714.

**Aubery, Claude.** <note>Latinized as *Alberti Claudii*</note> An exotic disputation on the **Concord of Physicians**, addressed to Wenceslaus Libavius of Ottenfeld, a Moravian. 8vo. Lausanne, 1585.

### Catalogue of Chymical Books

**Albertus Magnus,** on Women and the Births of Children, together with their medicines; also on the Virtue of several noble Herbs and on the Power of Gemstones; on the Kind and Nature of several Animals; together with a proven regimen against the Plague; newly improved by Q. Apolinaris <note>Q. Apolinaris was a pseudonym for the physician Walther Hermann Ryff (c. 1500–1548).</note>. With many figures. Quarto. Augsburg, 1608.

**The same,** by the same author. Octavo. Frankfurt am Main, 1572 and 1604.

**By the same author,** a little book on the *Secrets of Women* <note>original Latin: *de Secretis Mulierum*</note>. By the same author: a little book on the *Virtues of herbs, stones, and certain animals*. Also, on the *Minerals of the world*, and on certain effects caused by certain animals. We have added Michael Scot’s <note>Michael Scot (c. 1175–c. 1232) was a mathematician and scholar known in legend as a magician.</note> little work on the *Secrets of Nature*. Duodecimo. Strasbourg <note>original Latin: *Argentoratum*</note>, 1637.

**The same,** by the same author. Duodecimo. Amsterdam, 1702.

**Albertus Magnus’s Compendium,** or a short concept of Metals. Octavo. Hamburg, 1675. See <note>original Latin: *vid.*</note> *The Alchemical Seven Stars* <note>original German: *Alchymistisches Sieben Gestirn*, a famous collection of seven alchemical treatises published in 1675.</note>.

**By the same author,** Five Books on Minerals and Metallic things. Duodecimo. Cologne, 1569.

**The same,** by the same author. Octavo. Strasbourg, 1541. See Lully's Treatise on the *Secrets of Nature, or the Fifth Essence* <note>original Latin: *Quint. Essent.*</note>.

**Albertus Valentinus,** Physical Dissertation on the Shapes of various things in stones, and specifically on the Fossils of the County of Mansfeld. Respondent: Johannes Amandus Brunnerus. Quarto. Leipzig, 1675.

**Nathan Albineus,** *Contracted Chemical Library* <note>original Latin: *Bibliotheca Chemica Contracta*</note>, containing four treatises: (I) Johannes Aurelius Augurellus’s *Chrysopoeia* <note>The art of gold-making</note>, both the Greater and the Lesser, or the *Golden Fleece*; (II) The Cosmopolite’s (or Michael Sendivogius’s) *New Chemical Light*, consisting of two treatises, namely on Mercury and on Sulfur; (III) A Frenchman’s Anonymous *Handbook of Restored Physics*; (IV) An Anonymous (or Jean d'Espagnet’s) *Secret of Hermetic Philosophy*. Octavo. Geneva, 1653 and 1654.

...well-merited **Universal Medicine** <note>original German: *Universal-Artzney*; a "cure-all" or panacea</note> for the preservation of health and the prolongation of human life. Along with the perfect process and its preparation. Octavo <note>8.; a book size where the paper is folded three times to create eight leaves</note>. Dresden 1718.

**Fundamental and Experimental Art of Smelting** <note>original Latin: *Ars Fusoria fundamentalis & experimentalis*</note>, that is: thorough instruction, based on experience, regarding the **art of smelting**, or basic teaching on **copper smelting**, roasting, and **liquation** <note>term: *Seigern* (liquation); a metallurgical process of separating metals, specifically silver from copper, by heating them with lead</note>. Duodecimo <note>12.; a small, handheld book format</note>. Cassel 1689.

**On the Chemical Art**, two books, in which everything pertaining to the composition and use of the **philosopher’s stone** <note>original Latin: *lapidis sive pulveris philosophici*; the legendary substance capable of turning base metals into gold</note> or powder is briefly and clearly handed down. The first of these contains testimonies and theorems selected by **Robertus Vallensis** regarding the truth and antiquity of the chemical art and the powder or **medicine of the philosophers**, or **potable gold** <note>original Latin: *auri potabilis*; gold in a liquid form believed to have miraculous healing properties</note>. The second is a singular book by **Johannes Chrysippus Fanianus** on the art of metallic metamorphosis. Also included are the judgments and responses of ancient authors and especially legal scholars regarding the **legality of the alchemical art**, addressing the question: is alchemy a legitimate art? Octavo. Montbéliard <note>original: *Montisbeligardi*</note> 1602.

**On the Truth and Antiquity of the Chemical Art**, or the medicine of the philosophers, or potable gold: Testimonies and Theorems selected by Robertus Vallensis, [published] separately. Octavo. Leiden <note>original: *Lugduni Batavorum*</note> 1593.

The **Princes of the Chemical Art**, **Avicenna** and **Geber**, are contained in this volume. Octavo. Basel 1572.

**Artephius’s** **Key of Greater Wisdom** <note>original Latin: *Clavis Majoris Sapientiæ*; Artephius was a legendary figure to whom several important alchemical texts were attributed</note>, now published for the first time. Octavo. Paris 1609.

**The Art of Producing Gold** <note>original German: *Gold-Erzielungs-Kunst*</note> / dedicated to His Papal Holiness Leo X. Translated from Latin into German by M. Valentine Weigel. Octavo <note>8.; a book size where the paper is folded three times to create eight leaves</note>. Hamburg 1716.

**The works of Avicenna and Geber** <note>Avicenna (Ibn Sina) and Geber (Jabir ibn Hayyan) were the most influential early authorities on chemistry and medicine in the Islamic world and medieval Europe</note> are contained in this volume of the Princes of the chemical art; one of them has never before appeared in print, while the other has been compared with ancient manuscripts and illustrated with more elegant and numerous figures <note>illustrations or diagrams</note> than before. For the professors of this teaching, our edition has become both more pleasing and more useful. Octavo. Basel 1572.

**Homer’s Golden Chain.** <note>original Latin: *Aurea Catena homeri*; a highly influential Hermetic work describing the interconnectedness of all nature</note> Or a description of the origin of Nature and natural things: how and from what they are born and generated, and how they are destroyed back into their original, eternal essence. Also, what that "thing" is which gives birth to everything and destroys it again, shown in the simplest way according to Nature’s own guidance and order, and illustrated throughout with the most beautiful reasoning and causes. Octavo. Frankfurt and Leipzig 1723.

**The Golden Fleece** <note>original Latin: *Aureum Vellus*</note> or the Golden Treasure and Art-Chamber, containing the writings and books of the most distinguished and famous authors from the ancient treasure of the remaining and hidden relics and monuments of the kings and sages of the **Egyptians, Arabs, Chaldeans, and Assyrians**, arranged into special small treatises and brought into German by the highly enlightened philosopher **Salomon Trismosino** <note>Salomon Trismosin; a legendary alchemist and the purported teacher of Paracelsus</note>, also provided with many figures. Quarto <note>4.; a large book size where the paper is folded twice to create four leaves</note>. Hamburg 1708.

**By the same author**, under the title: **Revealed Secrets of the Philosopher’s Stone**, or the Treasure-Chamber of **Alchemy**. With many figures. Quarto. Same location 1718. **Note:** This book is entirely the same as the one above and unchanged, except for the title.

**The Second Volume by the same author.** Quarto. Basel 1604.

**Jacob Barner’s** *Philosophical Chemistry perfectly delineated, learnedly explained, and successfully demonstrated—much desired by many until now, but now dedicated to all lovers of medicine—along with a brief but accurate and fundamental doctrine of salts. Counsel on beginning, continuing, and completing medical studies.* Octavo. *Copenhagen 1628.*

brought to print by Friederich Roth-Scholtz. Octavo <note>8.; a book size formed by folding a sheet into eight leaves</note>. Nuremberg 1717.

Added at the end are a thousand hypotheses or chemical mixtures.* Octavo. Frankfurt 1669 and Leipzig 1681.

To which Johann Jakob Rosenstengel provided a preface, a synopsis of titles, marginal notes, distinctions of meanings and matters, and added notes and observations as well as an index; and subjoined Becher's Elements of Chemistry, freed from infinite errors, with supplements etc.* Duodecimo <note>12.; a smaller book format where the sheet is folded into twelve leaves</note>. Frankfurt 1705 and in Octavo 1716.

Frankfurt 1671. Published separately, and also in Becher’s *Subterranean Physics.*

Added for practice and as an example: II. The texture and anatomy of two great products, saltpeter and salt, and in confirmation of all the preceding...*

<meta>This page continues the bibliographic catalog of works by Johann Joachim Becher (1635–1682), specifically transitioning from his moral and political philosophy into his psychological and medical writings, followed by a major collection of his shorter chemical works edited by Friedrich Roth-Scholtz. ### Catalogue of Chemical Books.

...as well as what is treated in this little treatise; by J. F. R. <note>likely Johann Friedrich Roth-Scholtz</note>. Duodecimo <note>12.; a book size formed by folding a sheet into twelve leaves, resulting in a small pocket-sized volume</note>. 1706.

**By the same author, Psychosophia, or Wisdom of the Soul;** <note>original: Seelen-Weißheit</note> namely, how every human being, through the contemplation of their own soul alone, can thoroughly and constantly attain all science and wisdom. Along with a draft or invitation for a Philosophical Society. Duodecimo. Frankfurt 1683.

**By the same author, Aphorisms from the Medical Institutions of Sennert,** <note>Daniel Sennert (1572–1637) was a vastly influential physician who attempted to bridge the gap between Aristotelian philosophy and the newer chemical theories of Paracelsus</note> collected with great diligence. Duodecimo. Frankfurt 1663.

**By the same author, Rarer Chemical Works, Specifically:** <note>original: Opuscula Chymica Rariora</note> I. *The Hermetic Prophetic Tripod* <note>original: Tripus hermeticus fatidicus</note>, revealing chemical oracles, or the *Portable Laboratory*, with a truly spagyric <note>term: spagyric; alchemical medicine involving the separation and recombination of plant or mineral components</note> method of working, namely according to the requirements of nature. Added for practice and example is: II. *The Texture and Anatomy of the two great products, Saltpeter and Salt*, to which is joined for the confirmation of all the preceding: III. *The Mineral Alphabet*, or twenty-four theses on the origin, texture, and analysis of subterranean things and minerals. To these is added the *Concordance of Lunar Mercury* <note>original: concordantia mercurii lunæ; likely a reference to alchemical processes involving silver (Luna) and quicksilver</note>. IV. *Report on the Invention and Preparation of a Compendious Furnace*, which one can also conveniently carry with them on travels. Wherein is also added Colonel von Schellenberg's description of a *Universal Furnace*. V. *The Chemical Rose Garden*. VI. *Report on Sand*, as an everlasting metal or mine. VII. *Pantaleon Unmasked* <note>original: Pantaleon delarvatus; "Pantaleon" was the pseudonym of the alchemist Franz Gassmann, whom Becher sought to expose</note>. Added with a new preface and a most abundant index, and illustrated with many copperplate engravings by Friedrich Roth-Scholtz. Octavo <note>8.; a standard book size</note>. Nuremberg 1719.

**Beckers, Johann Gottfried, The Misuse of Natural Cinnabar** <note>original: Unfug des natürlichen Zinnobers; Cinnabar is mercury sulfide, often used as a pigment or in medicine, though its toxicity was a point of debate</note> in the human body. Quarto <note>4.; a large book size</note>. Copenhagen 1709.

Octavo. Hamburg 1672.

# Catalog of Chemical Books

[The] Art / by the most famous men who have ever labored in and written about the <term>chemical art: originally "Scheide-Kunst," literally the art of separation, the contemporary term for chemistry and alchemy</term> / such as *Schröder, Angelus Sala, Rolfinck, le Febure, Crollius, Charras, Beguin* and others still living / now presented to the public by a lover of the Art. Along with an addition on how base metals and common stones are to be improved / by Sir *Kenelm Digby*. Provided with various copperplate engravings / and translated from Dutch into High German. Octavo <note>8.; a book size where the printer's sheet is folded into eight leaves</note>. *Leipzig* 1694.

**Jacob Böhme,** otherwise called *Teutonicus* <note>original: "Teutonicus," a nickname meaning "the German [Philosopher]"</note> / *The Great Mystery* <note>original: "Mysterium Magnum"</note>, or an explanation of the First Book of *Moses* / concerning the revelation of the divine Word through the three <term>Principles: in Böhme's thought, these are the fundamental forces of Salt, Sulfur, and Mercury that govern both spiritual and material existence</term> of divine essence and the origin of the world and *Creation*, wherein the Realm of Nature / and the Realm of Grace are explained. Along with a biography of the *Author*. Quarto <note>4.; a medium-sized book format</note>. 1640.

*By the same author: An Idea of Adept Böhmean Chemistry* <note>original: "Idea chemiæ Böhmianæ adeptæ"</note>, a short outline of the preparation of the Philosopher's Stone. Duodecimo <note>12.; a small, pocket-sized book</note>. *Amsterdam* 1690.

*By the same author: Böhmean Metallurgy* <note>original: "Metallurgia Böhmiana"</note>, that is / a description of the metals according to their origin and essence / and how they are born from *Mercury, Salt* and *Sulfur*. Duodecimo. *In the same place* <note>original: "Ibidem," referring to Amsterdam</note> 1695.

**Jacob Böhme** Unmasked / or a thorough disclosure of how his so-called *Theosophical* writings are to be viewed / and from where such knowledge grew within him. With an attached *Dissertation on Adepts* / by *E. J. H., M.D.* Quarto. 1693.

**Dr. Johann Bohn’s** *Chemical-Physical Dissertations* <note>original: "Dissertationes chymico-physicæ"</note>, explaining the goals, instruments, and more frequent operations of chemistry, with an index of subjects and words, to which is added his previously published treatise on the *influence of air in the sublunary sphere* <note>original: "de aeris in sublunaris inflexu"; the sublunary sphere refers to the Earth and its atmosphere below the moon</note>. Quarto. Leipzig 1685.

*By the same author: A Letter to the most noble and distinguished Dr. Joel Langelott* regarding the insufficiency of alkali and acid to perform the role of principles or elements of natural bodies. Octavo. Leipzig 1675. See Becher’s *Subterranean Physics* <note>original: "Physica Subterranea," a foundational work by J.J. Becher on the composition of the earth and metals</note>.

**de Boodt, Anselmus Boëtius** *History of Gems and Stones, in which is shown not only [their] origin, nature, power, and price, but also the method by which oils, salts, tinctures, essences, secrets, and <term>magisteries: high-level chemical preparations intended to capture the "mastery" or full essence of a substance</term> can be crafted from them by the chemical art. **Borel, Pierre** <note>original: "Borelli Petri"</note> *The Chemical Library <note>original: "Bibliotheca Chymica"</note>, or a Catalog of hermetic philosophical books, in which are contained about four thousand chemical authors—concerning the transmutation of metals, mineral matters, and secrets—both manuscripts and published works, with their editions, up to the year 1653.* Duodecimo <note>12.; a small, pocket-sized book format</note>. *Paris 1654.*

**By the same author, the same work.** Duodecimo. *Heidelberg 1656.*

**Burnet, Duncan** <note>original: "Bornetti Duncani"</note> *Chemical Apprenticeship <note>original: "Tyrocinium chymicum"</note>,* that is, on the preparation and composition of Chemical Medicines. Translated from the Latin by the author himself. Octavo. Frankfurt am Main 1618.

**Bornitius, Jacob** <note>original: "Bornitii Jacobi"</note> *A Political Treatise on the sufficiency of things. I. On managing the Republic & the City. II. The cultivation of fields & metals. III. Crafts of every kind. IV. Trade by sea & land. V. Various ministries, with a most rich index of chapters, subjects, and words.* Quarto. *Frankfurt 1625.*

# Catalog of Chemical Books.

**Ole Borch,** *The Wisdom of Hermes of the Egyptians and of the Chemists, defended against the remarks of Hermann Conring.* <note>original: "Hermetis Ægyptiorum & chemicorum sapientia ab Hermanni Conringii animadverſionibus vindicata"</note> Quarto <note>4.; a medium-sized book format where the paper is folded twice to create four leaves</note>. *Copenhagen 1674.*

**By the same [author],** *Metallic Assaying taught clearly and concisely.* <note>original: "Docimaſticè metallica clarè, & compendiario tradita"; docimastics is the historical term for testing the purity of metals</note> Quarto. *Copenhagen 1677.*

**By the same [author],** *Dissertation on the Origin and Progress of Chemistry.* <note>original: "de ortu & Progreſſu chymiæ diſſertatio"</note> Quarto. *Ibid. [Copenhagen] 1668.*

**By the same [author],** *The Art of Metallic Assaying, translated into German by Georg Kas.* <note>original: "Metalliſche Probier-Kunſt / verteutſcht durch Georg Kas"</note> Octavo <note>8.; a standard book size where the paper is folded into eight leaves</note>. *Copenhagen 1680.*

**By the same [author],** *Two Volumes of Academic Dissertations, preceded by the funeral oration of Paulus Vindingius on the passing of the Blessed Ole Borch.* <note>original: "Diſſertationum Academicarum Tomi II. cui præmittitur Pauli Vindingii Oratio parentalis in exceſſum B. D. Olai Borrichii"</note> Quarto. *Copenhagen 1714.*

**By the same [author],** *A Short Concept of the Use of Native Herbs in Medicine, to which is attached an extract of herbs and a special kind of tea, translated from Latin into German by Johann Reese.* <note>original: "Kurtzer Begriff vom Gebrauch der Einländiſchen Kräuter in der Artzney..."</note> Octavo. *Hamburg 1696.*

**By the same [author],** *A Survey of Illustrious Chemical Writers, a posthumous booklet preceded by a history of his life written by himself.* <note>original: "Conſpectus ſcriptorum chemicorum illuſtriorum..."</note> Quarto. *Copenhagen 1697.*

**By the same [author],** *Dissertation on the Generation of Stones in the Macro- and Microcosm. To which is added a supplement by Giuseppe Lanzoni.* <note>original: "Diſſertatio de lapidum generatione in macro- & microcosmo"; in this context, the macrocosm is the world/nature, and the microcosm is the human body, referring to both geological stones and kidney/bladder stones</note> Duodecimo <note>12.; a small portable book format</note>. *Ferrara 1687.*

**By the same [author],** *Handbook on the Use of Native Plants in Medicine and, at the end, on the "clyssus" of plants and a specific tea.* <note>original: "de uſu plantarum... de clyſſo plantarum"; a "clyssus" was an alchemical term for a condensed extract or vapor containing the "essential powers" of a substance</note> Quarto. *Copenhagen 1690.*

**Robert Boyle,** *The Sceptical Chymist, or Physico-Chemical Doubts and Paradoxes concerning the Spagyric Principles, commonly called Hypostatical, as they are usually proposed and defended by the Crowd of Alchemists, preceded by a part of another dissertation on the same subject.* <note>original: "Chymiſta ſcepticus vel dubia & paradoxa chymico-phyſica circa ſpagyricorum principia, vulgò dicta hypoſtatica..."; "Hypostatical principles" refers to the three alchemical elements of salt, sulfur, and mercury</note> Duodecimo. *Rotterdam 1668.*

**By the same [author],** *The Joyous Noctiluca, or several new phenomena, along with an instruction on how to prepare all kinds of phosphorus and self-shining beings. Translated from English into German by J. L. M. C.* <note>original: "die luſtige Nocticula... Phosphoros und ſelbſt ſcheinende Weſen"; Noctiluca refers to substances that glow in the dark</note> Octavo. *Hamburg 1682.*

**Dr. Thomas Browne,** *Pseudodoxia Epidemica,* <note>original: "Common Errors"</note> that is: An Investigation into those errors that are prevalent among the common people and elsewhere. Composed in seven books in such a way that it treats, at the beginning, errors in general, with the addition of various small treatises, such as a manual of the restored Art of Nature—containing the foundation of the entire chemical science, etc.—and then deals with those errors concerning minerals, plants, animals, humans, images and paintings, and descriptions of the world and history. With many figures. Quarto. *Nuremberg 1680.*

**Johann Jacob Brückmann and Johann Heinrich Weber,** *Newly Invented Elementary Machine or Universal Means for all kinds of Water-Raising,* by which one can drive all kinds of mill-machinery without wind, without rivers, and without the strength of humans or animals; in particular, all obstacles in mines arising either from a lack of surface water or an excess of ground water can be conveniently removed from the depths. Quarto. *Kassel 1720.*

### in the Roth-Scholtz Library.

**Bruschius, Caspar,** *Thorough Description of the Fichtelberg Mountain,* from which four navigable waters—the Main, the Eger, the Naab, and the Saale—arise; therein many ancient histories are explained. Also a clear description of the Eger River and all the waters flowing into it and the neighboring villages, especially also the tin mine of Schlackenwald, which is called "the Hub" <note>original: "die Hub"; a specific local name for this mining district in Bohemia</note>, and how the same is to be found at the present time. **Bünting, Johann Philipp,** *Subterranean Forest,* <note>original: "Sylva Subterranea"</note> or the excellent utility of the underground forest of stone and coal. Duodecimo. *Halle* 1693.

S.,** *Remains as Witnesses of the Deluge,* <note>original: "Rudera diluvii testes"</note> that is: Signs and witnesses of the Great Flood, regarding the current state of our terrestrial and aquatic globe; in particular, those various formerly vanished animals and plants found recently in different places within the Querfurt district, considered by the light of natural wisdom. With many copperplates. Quarto. *Leipzig* 1710.

By the same author: *Subterranean Coralliography;* <note>original: "Coralliographia Subterranea"</note> or a dissertation on fossil corals, specifically on hornstone <note>original: "lapide corneo, Horn oder gemeinen Feuer-Stein"; refers to varieties of flint or chert</note>, with copper plates and figures. ### Catalogue of Chemical Books

...a Nomenclature of the most numerous observations, dedicated to the Illustrious Senators of Bologna and the most wise Presidents of the University of the same City. 24. *Bologna* 1657.

**Burggrav, Johann Ernst,** *Achilles Brought Back to Life Fully Armed,* <term>panoplos</term> <note>original Greek: πανοπλόϲ, meaning "completely armed." Burggrav uses the hero Achilles as a metaphor for a powerful chemical or medicinal preparation.</note> or a Physical-Volcanic Suit of Armor, which is led forth into battle as a sacred and inviolable weapon against the enemy for the <term>lover of armor</term>. <note>original Greek: φιλο-πλόϲ</note> To which is prefixed the *Conjectural Judgment* <note>original Greek: Ἐπίκριϲιϲ ϲτοχα-ϲτική</note> of **Marcellus Vranckheim** regarding the Achilles *who fights with all-superior weapons*. <note>original Greek: πανυπεροπλόμαχον</note> Octavo. *Amsterdam* 1612.

**C**abala: *The Mirror of Art and Nature in Alchemy,* <note>original: "Cabala Speculum Artis & Naturæ in Alchymia"</note> showing what the wisdom of the most ancient Sages is, what the substance may be, which exists as a triple and yet single stone. With copperplate engravings. Quarto. *Augsburg* 1704.
The same in *German* with copperplates. Quarto. *Augsburg* 1704.
The same in *German* without copperplates. Quarto. *Augsburg* 1616.

*A Truer Description of the Cabala,* <note>original: "Cabalæ verior deſcriptio"</note> that is: A thorough description and proof of all natural and supernatural things; how all was created through the Word *Fiat* <note>Latin: "Let it be done," referring to the divine command in Genesis</note> and thereafter generated, nourished, and corrupted through the Center of Heaven and Earth. Octavo. *Hamburg* 1680.

**Cesalpino, Andrea,** *Three Books on Metals.* <note>original Latin: "de Metallicis Libri III." Cesalpino was a famous Italian physician and botanist who classified minerals and plants.</note> Quarto. *Nuremberg* 1602.

**Calckhoff, Johann Christoph,** *Dissertation on the Transmutation of Metals,* which **Caspar Cramer** will dispute for his License <note>referring to the *Licentiate* degree, an academic step between a Master's and a Doctorate</note>. Quarto.

**Camerarius, Joachim,** *The Medical and Philosophical Garden,* in which are contained brief descriptions of many plants, not a few new images, indications of their native places, peculiar observations on their cultivation, and furthermore several easily-obtained remedies <note>original Latin: "euporiſta," referring to medicines made from common, accessible ingredients</note>, as well as certain philological matters. Likewise, the *Hercynian Forest,* <note>original: "Sylva Hercynia," referring to the ancient forest region in central Germany, specifically the Harz mountains</note> or a catalogue of plants growing spontaneously in most of the mountains and places of the Hercynian Forest facing Saxony, compiled with singular zeal by **Johann Thal,** Physician of Nordhausen. With copperplate engravings. Quarto. *Frankfurt am Main* 1588.

**Camerarius, Elias,** *Three Dissertations,* presenting: I. The natural and preternatural state of the Animal Spirits <note>In historical medicine, "animal spirits" were believed to be subtle vapors or fluids that traveled through the nerves to facilitate movement and sensation</note> on the occasion...

# Catalog of Chemical Books.

...derived from the ethereal, vegetable, and mineral [kingdoms], namely of the forests, gardens, meadows, plants, flowers, rivers, mountains, animals, elements, stars, metals, minerals, humans, and other creatures; their properties, effects, and virtues. These are proposed as adapted to the three ranks of the republic—ecclesiastical, political, and economic—and are explained through theological, ethical, political, physical, and metaphysical meditations and most pleasant dissertations, on account of the remarkable utility they contain. Octavo. Frankfurt, 1630.

**Cassius, the Medical Sage** <note>original: "Iatrosophistæ," a Greek-derived term for a philosopher-physician</note>, **Natural and Medical Questions** (84 in number) concerning the nature of man and various diseases, interpreted by Conrad Gesner, physician of Zurich, now published for the first time. The same in Greek, far more corrected than before, with certain commentaries. To these is added a most accurate **Catalog of Simple and Readily Available Medicines** <note>original: "Medicamentorum simplicium & parabilium"</note> that oppose the poison of the plague <note>original: "pestilentiæ veneno"</note>, mentioned by all the most famous writers, both ancient and modern. With a brief instruction on how to use them, the likes of which has been written by no one else until now, by the author Antonius Schneberger. Octavo. Zurich, 1562.

**Cassius, Andreas**, on that final and most perfect work of nature and the prince of earthly stars, **Gold**; on its wonderful nature, generation, characteristics, effects, and its suitability for the operations of the [alchemical] art. Thoughts illustrated by the most noble experiments. Octavo. Hamburg, 1685. <note>Andreas Cassius was a famous physician and chemist known for "Purple of Cassius," a pigment created from gold</note>

**Castellus, Petrus**, an **Examination** <note>original: "εξέτασις" (exetasis), a Greek term for inquiry or examination</note> **of the Scented Hyena**. Adorned with copperplate figures. Duodecimo. Frankfurt, 1668. <note>The "scented hyena" refers to the civet, an animal then prized in pharmacy and perfumery for its musk</note>

**The Chemical Cato** <note>original: "Cato-chemicus." The name "Cato" refers to the Roman statesman known for his stern moral standards; here it implies a "judge" of true chemistry</note>, a treatise in which the characteristics of true and genuine Hermetic philosophy, as well as those of painted and sophisticated pseudo-chemistry and the masters of both, are accurately delineated. Duodecimo. Hamburg, 1690.

**The Works of Moses Charas**, Volume Three, containing the natural history of animals, plants, and minerals which are ingredients in the composition of the **Theriac of Andromachus** <note>A famous ancient "universal" medicine and antidote containing dozens of ingredients</note>. Includes experiments concerning the **Viper**. With an added series of new experiments. Quarto. Geneva, 1674.

->In the Roth-Scholtz Library.<-

**Checcacci, Andrea**, **Reflections, or Observations on the books regarding the History and Causes of Plants by Theophrastus** <note>Theophrastus was a student of Aristotle and is often called the 'father of botany'</note> **written by Domenico Vigna**; these were made and observed regarding trees, shrubs, sub-shrubs, stalks, plants, and herbs, all of which are difficult to name, and are reduced as much as possible to their Italian and common names. Quarto. Pisa, 1625.

**Chrysander, Alethophilus** <note>A pseudonym meaning 'Lover of Truth'</note>, **The Golden Age Revealed** <note>original: "Aureum Seculum Patefactum"</note>, or the opened golden time, in which the **Universal Monster** or **Chaotic Matter** <note>original: "monstruum universale seu materia chaotica," referring to the 'First Matter' used in alchemy to create the Philosopher's Stone</note>, long desired by all chemists and true philosophers, is discovered as clear as the sun. Octavo. Nuremberg, 1706.

**Chemical** <note>original: "Chymica"</note> **Curious Collection**, containing the true anatomy or analysis of natural things from the triple kingdom—vegetable, animal, and mineral, etc. By the work and study of J. D. Thom. A. Quarto. Frankfurt, 1693.

**The Incomparable Gold Mine of Chemistry** <note>original: "Chymiæ Aurifodina incomparabilis"</note>, which is revealed by the prelude of the magic nights of the past six months; the **Chemical Sibylline Lots**; the uprooted pomegranate of Vanni; by immortal adept authors, to which is added a commentary on the **Universal Medicine** <note>original: "pharmaco catholico"</note>. With copper plate figures. Quarto. Leiden, 1696 and Amsterdam, 1666.

**Rational Chemistry** <note>original: "Chymia Rationalis"</note>, that is, a rational instruction on how, by means of the **Spagyric Art** <note>term: spagyric; the alchemical process of separating and recombining the essence of plants or minerals for medicine</note>, the most common medicines of the present time are to be prepared from the three kingdoms of nature. To which is added **Chemical Practice** <note>original: "Praxis Chymiatrica"</note>, or a short yet clear instruction on how the most prominent diseases of the human body can be sufficiently recognized from their causes and signs, and successfully cured with the previously shown medicines. Translated from English into German. Octavo. Frankfurt and Leipzig, 1696.

**A Specimen of Natural Chemistry**, in which it is clearly evident that no process occurs in chemical laboratories to which a similar or analogous one does not occur in the body of an animal. Octavo. The Hague, 1707.

**Curious Chemistry**, adorned with various experiments not only from the vegetable kingdom but also from the mineral and animal. Octavo. London, 1687.

**Chemical Writings**, three curious ones, namely: 1. Nicolaus Soleas's **Philosophical Principles**. 2. Mr. C. L. B. L.'s **Chemical Catechism**. 3. 130 **Principles** translated from Tuscan <note>The dialect of Tuscany, Italy</note> into German. Octavo. Hof, 1723.

**A Chemical Something in Nothing**, that is: How the highly renowned **Philosopher's Stone** <note>The legendary substance capable of turning base metals into gold and curing all diseases</note> as a noble gift from God is removed and sought in vain in high things, but is fortunately found in "something." Octavo. Dresden, 1722.

**Chemical Revelation of True Wisdom**, that is, a faithful and sincere discovery of the **Matter** <note>The 'Prima Materia' or starting material required for the Great Work</note> which must be taken if one wishes to make the true **Philosopher's Stone** <note>original: "Lapidem Philosophorum"</note> or **Universal Tincture** <note>original: "tinctura universalem," a liquid form of the Philosopher's Stone</note>. Shown from many Theophrastian <note>Referring to the followers of Paracelsus, whose name was Theophrastus von Hohenheim</note> manuscripts and published in print by J. J. Chymiphilus. Octavo. Nuremberg, 1720.

**Chemical Double-Leaf**, that is, two excellent chemical treatises. The first: **An Opened Entrance to the King's Closed Palace** <note>A famous alchemical text often attributed to Eirenaeus Philalethes, describing the path to the Stone</note>. The other: **On the Philosopher's Stone**, how it should be rightly prepared, by Brother Ferrari the Monk <note>original: "Fraeris Ferrarii Monachi"</note>. Both translated for the first time into German by Johannes Lange. Octavo. Frankfurt and Hamburg, 1674. See Philalethes.

**Clauder, Gabriel**, **Dissertation on the Universal Tincture or Philosopher's Stone**, in which is described through proofs and experiences: 1. What this may be; 2. Whether it is found in Nature, and whether it is useful for a Christian to investigate it; 3. From what kind of **Matter**; and 4. How it is prepared. To this, other useful and curious matters have been added, along with a treatise dealing with **Air-Gold** <note>Likely referring to a spiritual or volatile essence of gold extracted from the atmosphere</note>. Octavo. Nuremberg, 1682.

**Claveus**, **Defense of Gold-Making and Silver-Making** <note>original: "Apologia Chrysopoeæ & Argyropoeæ"</note> against Thomas Erastus, in which it is disputed and taught whether, what, and how gold-making and silver-making exist. With a treatise on the triple preparation of gold and silver. Octavo. Oberursel <note>original: "Ursellis"</note>, 1601.

**By the Same Author**, **The German Claveus** <note>A pun on the author's name, Claveus, and the Latin word 'clavis' meaning 'key'</note>, that is, a precious little book...

...extensive Philosophical Treatise, which contains within itself the true practice <note>original: "Praxin"</note> and the active part of philosophy for obtaining the Philosopher's Stone. <term>Philosopher's Stone</term><note>The legendary "Lapis Philosophorum," a substance believed to turn base metals into gold and grant eternal life.</note> Along with the ways to prepare the Mineral Stone and the <term>calcination</term><note>A process of heating a substance to high temperatures in air or oxygen, often to reduce it to a powder.</note> of metals. To which is added a work by St. Dunstan <note>St. Dunstan (909–988) was an Archbishop of Canterbury who later alchemical traditions claimed was a master of the craft.</note> on the Philosopher's Stone, together with the curious experiments of Rhumelius, and the preparations of Angelus Sala, both very famous chemists <note>original: "Chymici"</note> in their time. **Colbovius, Peter.** A well-intentioned Chemical Poem <note>original: "Chymisch Carmen"</note> concerning various still unknown universal <term>Alkahest</term> <note>A hypothetical "universal solvent" capable of dissolving every other substance, including gold.</note> menstruums <note>solvents</note> for the various "Chaos of the Philosophers" <note>A term for the raw material or "Prima Materia" used at the start of the alchemical process.</note> and the Philosopher's Stone. Octavo. Dresden, 1667.

**Communication of an Excellent Chemical Medicine.** By the power of which, next to GOD and a good diet, the famous nobleman Fredericus Gualdus preserved his life for 400 years unto these our times, and was recently seen in the year 1688 in Venice. <note>Federico Gualdi was a mysterious figure in the 17th century rumored to be an immortal alchemist.</note> Translated from English and Italian. To which is added a treatise: *The Unmasked Gualdus*, or Frederick Gualdus convicted of lies and imposture by himself; <note>original: "Der Entlarvte Gualdus, sive Frid. Gualdus ex se ipso mendacii & imposturæ convictus"</note> that is, an extensive proof that what is claimed of a 400-year-old Venetian nobleman and his medicine is to be held more as a fable than a true history. Duodecimo. Augsburg, 1700.

**Concius, M. Andreas.** A Physical Discourse on the Philosopher’s Stone, which is otherwise called the *lapis Philosophorum*, along with other matters arising from this, all proven with philosophical grounds. Quarto. <note>A "Quarto" (4.) is a medium-sized book, roughly square.</note>

This refers to a legendary figure in alchemical literature, sometimes associated with simple, "rustic" laboratory methods.</note>, or the diverse opinions on the Little Peasant's Particular; consisting of and presented in fifteen processes dealing with it, from which the art-loving reader will perceive the agreement and disagreement of the authors thereof; along with two other very curious particulars concerning the **exaltation of the sun** <note>The purification or "raising" of gold to its highest state.</note> and the extraction of its soul, consisting of over 30 of the best processes, to which, for the sake of curiosity, the *Chemical Heaven* <note>original: "coelum chemicum"</note> of **Jacobus Tollius** has also been added. Octavo. Leipzig 1715.

In early modern medicine, "simples" referred to individual natural ingredients—herbs, minerals, or animal products—used to create medicinal compounds.</note>, in which simples—namely vegetables, minerals, and animals commonly used in pharmacies—are described according to both ancient and modern authors, and applied to medical practice. Leiden 1618.

**de Cordua, Franciscus.** *Scriptural and Rational Thoughts on Treasure-Hunting and the Conjuring of Spirits.* Translated from Latin into German. 8vo. Hamburg 1716.

<meta>This page contains the "Vorrede" (Preface) to the second part of Friedrich Roth-Scholtz’s bibliographic project. It continues from the previous page's title announcement for the second volume (1727), transitioning from the formal title to the author’s personal address to his readers regarding the delays and future plans for the series.</meta>

Catalogue of Chemical Books.
Cro-

# Preface.

## Highly Esteemed Reader!

It is true that this time, the second part of the commenced catalog of my **Chemical Library** has remained behind somewhat longer than I had expected due to various obstacles. I hope, however, that the parts still missing—as well as the supplements and the index—will soon follow, if **God** grants life and health.

The chemical writings of **Nicholas Flamel** <note>original: "Nicolai Flammelli"; Flamel (c. 1330–1418) was a French scribe who achieved posthumous fame as an alchemist credited with discovering the Philosopher's Stone.</note>.
The chemical writings of **George Ripley** <note>original: "Georgii Riplaei"; Ripley (c. 1415–1490) was an influential English Augustinian canon and alchemist.</note>.
And the **Assembly of the Philosophers** <note>original: "Turba Philosophorum," one of the oldest Latin alchemical texts, written as a transcript of a meeting of pre-Socratic philosophers discussing the creation of the Stone.</note>; that is, the book of the golden art, alongside other authors, which together comprise 36 books, in which the finest and most ancient philosophers are gathered. They all treat unanimously of the **Universal Medicine** <note>also known as the Panacea or Elixir, a substance capable of curing all diseases and prolonging life</note>, divided into two parts and adorned with beautiful figures. Published by **Philipp Morgenstern** of Eisleben <note>original: "Philippum Morgenstern, Islebiensem"</note>, in octavo <note>original: "8."; referring to the book size where a sheet is folded into eight leaves</note>. These works, which I am preparing for print and for which I am already having the necessary copperplates engraved, shall appear very shortly.

->Nuremberg, the<-
->21st of January 1727.<-

<summary>In this preface, Friedrich Roth-Scholtz apologizes to his readers for the publication delay of the second part of his bibliography and announces forthcoming editions of major alchemical works by Nicholas Flamel and George Ripley.</summary>
<keywords>Friedrich Roth-Scholtz, preface, chemical bibliography, Nicholas Flamel, George Ripley, Turba Philosophorum, Universal Medicine, Philipp Morgenstern, book printing, Nuremberg</keywords>

Oswald Croll’s <note>original: "Crolii Oswaldi"</note> **Chemical Basilica** <note>original: "Basilica Chymica"; a foundational text of iatrochemistry (chemical medicine), first published in 1609</note>, expanded with many select and most secret descriptions approved by personal manual experience, and the use of the most select chemical remedies, by Johannes Hartmann, Doctor of Medicine and formerly the most celebrated Professor of **Chymiatry** <term>Chymiatria: the early modern practice of "chemical medicine" or iatrochemistry, which sought to treat diseases using mineral and chemical substances rather than just plants</term> at the University of Marburg, and Chief Physician to the Princes of Hesse. Published by Johannes Michaelis, Doctor of Philosophy and Medicine, and Georg Everhard Hartmann, the author's son. Octavo <note>original: "8."; a book size where each sheet of paper is folded into eight leaves</note>. Geneva, at the expense of Pierre Chouët, 1635 and 1630.

By the same author: **Chemical Basilica**, or **Alchemical Royal Jewel**: A philosophical description, confirmed and verified by his own personal experience, including the use of the most excellent chemical medicines taken from the light of grace and nature. Along with his attached treatise on the internal **Signatures** <term>Signatures: the "Doctrine of Signatures" was the belief that plants or minerals that resemble parts of the body can treat ailments of those parts</term> or signs of things. Quarto <note>original: "4."; a book size where each sheet is folded into four leaves</note>. Frankfurt, [published] by Gottfried Tampach.

By the same author: **D. O. M. A.** <note>original Latin abbreviation for "Deo Optimo Maximo Altissimo," meaning "To God, the Best, Greatest, and Highest"</note> **Crollius Revived**, or the **Hermetic Miracle Tree**, wherein it can be seen how the wondrous works of **God** are to be rightly understood and recognized by lovers of chemical medicines, etc., gathered from the great **Hermetic** <term>Hermetic: relating to the mystical and alchemical traditions attributed to the legendary figure Hermes Trismegistus</term> pleasure garden and divided into seven booklets. With many figures <note>illustrations</note>. Quarto. Frankfurt am Main, 1635. *See Croll’s German Chemical Basilica.*

By the same author: The same. With many figures. Quarto. In the same place, [published] by Hans Friederich Weiss, 1647.

**The Most Hidden Secrets of the Philosophy of the Ancients** <note>original French: "le Secrets les plus cachez de la Philosophie des aciens"</note> discovered and explained following a most curious history. By Monsieur Crosset, of La Haumène. Duodecimo <note>original: "12."; a small book size</note>. At Paris, 1723.

Michael Crügner’s **Chemical Gardening**, <note>original: "Crügneri, Michaelis Chymischer Garten-Bau"</note> that is: A **Spagyric** <term>Spagyric: a method of herbal medicine involving alchemical processes of separation and recombination, intended to enhance the plant's medicinal properties</term> description of thirty-four kinds of plants...

# Catalogue of Chemical Books

...wax and herbs / according to correct fundamental instruction / which are generated from <term>Putrefaction</term> <note>In alchemy, the process of decomposition or "rotting" was considered the essential first stage of transformation and rebirth.</note> and <term>Transplantation</term> <note>The theory that the "virtue" or life-force of one organism could be transferred into another.</note> / diligently observed by the ever-seeking Author. Along with a small <term>Haliographia</term> <note>A treatise on the nature and extraction of salts.</note>. etc. Quarto <note>original: "4."; a book format where sheets are folded into four leaves, creating a medium-sized volume.</note>. Nuremberg 1653.

By the same P. M. C. <note>Paul Michael Crügner</note>, newly expanded **Chemical Spring**. That is: A remarkable Medico-Chemical Treatise, including an Astrological Continuation. How to collect plants / and properly prepare them for certain illnesses. Therein especially, briefly and faithfully shown / what are not only erroneous opinions and false processes, but also on the contrary / correct / brief and true processes, demonstrated with remarkable manual techniques / along with added use and benefit. All observed / tested / demonstrated and described with the highest diligence / personal experience / and laborious research by the Author in Dresden. Primarily for the benefit and pleasure of all lovers of truth / and those devoted to the right Hermetic medicine. Prefaced by the *Informatorium Medico-Chymicum* or Instruction / on what a true Chemical or Hermetic Physician is / and what is required of him. Quarto. Nuremberg, published by Wolfgang the Younger / and Johann Andreas Endter, in the year 1654.

By the same P. M. C., **Chemical Summer**. That is: A remarkable Medico-Chemical Treatise, in which is especially briefly and faithfully demonstrated / how plants are to be collected according to the right influence and right measure of the heavenly / properly divided <term>Zodiac</term> <note>original: "Zodiaci"; Crügner emphasizes harvesting herbs when the sun and planets are in specific astrological signs to maximize their healing power.</note> / and then further how they are to be properly prepared chemically and astrologically / as well as showing the correct use and benefit. Quarto. Nuremberg, published by Wolfgang the Younger / and Johann Andreas Endter, 1656.

It is said that this Michael Crügner also published the following / which I have not yet seen at this time:

**Culpeper, Nicholas** Testament or Last Will / which he left and commanded to his spouse for the common benefit / wherein choice and very useful secrets are revealed / which lay hidden beneath his heart / while he was still alive / and which he did not wish to bring to public light before his death / containing within it all sorts of strange and wonderful experiments in various arts / especially in the **Art of Medicine and Healing** / how one should prepare and make all sorts of medicines / such as waters / syrups / oils / <term>Electuaries</term> <note>Medicinal pastes made by mixing powders with honey or syrup.</note> / preserves / salts / pills / purging-drinks and <term>lozenges</term> <note>original: "Küchlein"; literally "little cakes," but in a medical context, these are troches or lozenges.</note>. Along with two separate little treatises, one of which deals with fevers / the other with the plague: as well as uncommon and choice medical rules and recipes / given so that the commonest understanding can grasp and comprehend it. Printed for the fifth time in the English language / to which fifth edition 200 choice recipes have been added. Translated from English into German. Octavo. <note>original: "8."; a smaller book format.</note> Hamburg 1675.
**Cygnæus, Eucharius** Spectacles <note>original Latin: "Conspicilium"</note> serving for the notice of sick eyes which refuse to bear the light of truth by reason of the subject, object, medium, and end; opposed to the futile admonition of Heinrich Neuhusius concerning the **Brothers of the R. C.** <note>The Rosicrucians, a mysterious spiritual and cultural movement.</note>. Whether they exist? What kind they are? From whence they took that name? And for what purpose they spread such fame? And exhibited to the Sons of Doctrine from fame, confession, and the fountain of truth. Octavo. 1619.
**Dale, Samuel** Pharmacology or Introduction to the **Medical Matter** <note>original Latin: "Materia Medica"; the body of collected knowledge about the therapeutic properties of any substance used for healing.</note>, in which the simple official medicaments, that is Minerals, Vegetables, Animals and their parts used in medical shops <note>original Latin: "officinis"; refers to the 'officine' or apothecary shop.</note>, are arranged into a natural method, succinctly and accurately...
